# Formalizing decision problem consistency and coherence

**Summary.** This extended technical conversation develops a formal framework for decision problems and calibration in decision theory, addressing inconsistencies in the philosophical treatment of choices under uncertainty. Through iterative refinement of mathematical definitions, the participants work through problems like Transparent Newcomb, Smoking Lesion, and custom decision trees (the Prophet problem) to formalize the notion of when decision procedures are consistent with decision problems. Major contributions include a tree-based semantics for decision problems, multiple calibration notions (strict observation, masked, limit-calibration, and subjective-state calibration), and exploration of how these frameworks handle paradoxical scenarios and fixed-point selection in self-referential situations.

**Where to look:**
- 1-5: Initial request to formalize decision problem consistency; detailed discussion of post structure and identified type-signature issues with dependent types
- 6-7: Claude provides comprehensive artifact with formal definitions of decision problems as finite query trees, multiple calibration variants, and propositions about transparent Newcomb variants and smoking lesion
- 8-11: the author critiques the finiteness restriction, requests Jeffrey-Bolker framework integration, queries whether actions should be events, and raises the conceptual problem of fixed-point selection in Newcomb-like problems with self-referential action probabilities
- 12-40: Detailed technical discussion of how to represent causal structure within Jeffrey-Bolker framework, the role of observations vs subjective states in decision-points, and whether the tree format can handle arbitrary counterfactual dependence or must restrict to finite structures
- 41-75: Extended working session on specific decision problem examples (prophet/told-you-so, transparent Newcomb variants V1-V3), value table calculations showing when different policies dominate, and verification of calibration properties across different scenarios
- 76-96: Deep dive into trembling-hand refinements and limit-calibration, exploring whether Definition 10 (limit-calibration) is sufficient to rule out EDT taking dominated actions in the five-and-ten problem, with analysis of state vs procedure-level constraints
- 97-100: Resolution of whether trembling-EDT could force rejection of dominated action selection; formalization of tremble-consistency as a separate meta-level property distinct from calibration itself, with reference to trembling-hand perfection from game theory

## What is a decision problem?



I recently spent some time [thinking about the definition of a decision problem](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/CDkbYSFTwggGE8mWp/coherent-care), inspired by Nate Soare's remarks in [Decisions are for making bad outcomes inconsistent](https://intelligence.org/2017/04/07/decisions-are-for-making-bad-outcomes-inconsistent/). The aim is to make "decision theory is systematized winning" or "why ain'cha rich?" arguments very clear, in a way that avoids the common response "rich by whose measure?" (that is: the definition of optimality is part of the subject matter of decision theories, so it cannot be used to judge between them).



It seems like there is an "academic perspective" which can be observed across several arguments against "MIRI-style" decision theory (functional decision theory, updateless decision theory, timeless decision theory, etc). This academic perspective can be observed in the contrary view expressed in decisions-are-for-making-bad-outcomes-inconsistent, as well as Will MacAskill's [A Critique of Functional Decision Theory](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ySLYSsNeFL5CoAQzN/a-critique-of-functional-decision-theory), and other places. The academic perspective classifies many threats as [non-credible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-credible_threat) because following through on the threat is costly, even if the benefit (others not crossing you) is worth the cost on net.



"Decisions are for making bad outcomes inconsistent" means something like: you should not judge a decision procedure poorly for behavior which avoids bad outcomes. This is most obvious for outright inconsistencies. Some decision problems, such as Smoking Lesion, are inconsistent with some decision procedures, such as EDT. (Either the agent's decision procedure is not as described, or the decision problem is not as described.) The academic perspective judges a decision theory for its advice in such cases _anyway_, whereas I am inclined to say that if a decision procedure is inconsistent with a decision problem, then it avoids the problem, and cannot be judged negatively for it. I'd also extend this principle to probabilistically avoiding bad outcomes. For example, in Transparent Newcomb with an imperfect predictor (appropriately spelled out), one-boxing _probabilistically avoids_ seeing an empty box. The academic perspective critiques the one-boxing choice myopically: _whatever the case_ (whether the box is empty or full), two-boxing is higher expected utility. This analysis doesn't care about the fact, inherent to the description of the decision problem, that one-boxing makes the bad empty-box outcome less probable.



One way of understanding the academic perspective is that it conflates the subjective state of the agent with the decision problem. If a decision problem _is just_ a subjective state, then there's no way to express the idea that decision procedures can allow agents to avoid ending up in specific situations. It isn't natural to ask whether an agent who follows a given decision procedure can even consistently be in such a situation.



Therefore, to make these ideas clear, I want to more accurately define "decision problem" and clarify the role decision problems play in the activity of decision theory. It seems crucial that decision problems are something we "put agents into" (something like a function which takes an agent and yields a world). It also seems crucial that a decision problem can involve multiple decision-points.



My formalism and arguments could use some work. In particular, I gave the type of a decision procedure as $(S,A) \to A$. The two $A$s are not really the same; the first one is a set of actions, while the second one is an individual action from that set. (This should have been formalized with a dependent type.)



One thing I want to make clearer is the distinction between decision problems as I'm discussing them vs learning theory. Vanessa Kosoy has expressed the idea that, to properly analyze a decision problem, we need to understand how an agent could _learn_ that it is in that situation:



> Finally, the decision-theoretic paradoxes are a more equivocal issue than it seems, because the usual philosophical way of thinking about decision theory assumes that the model of the environment is given, whereas in our way of thinking, the model is learned. This is important: for example, if AIXI is placed in a repeated Newcomb's problem, it will learn to one-box, since its model will predict that one-boxing causes the money to appear inside the box. In other words, AIXI might be regarded as a CDT, but the learned "causal" relationships are not the same as physical causality. 

> ([The Learning-Theoretic AI Alignment Agenda](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5bd75cc58225bf0670375575/the-learning-theoretic-ai-alignment-research-agenda#Towards_a_rigorous_definition_of_intelligence))





I like the sentiment, but what I'm trying to do here sets aside the learning-theoretic issues and focuses on "pure decision theory". I think the learning-theoretic perspective is superior in some sense, but I also think we can make many of the same points in a pure decision-theoretic way.



Vanessa's infra-bayesianism seems like the main 'competitor' to contrast my theory of decision problems against. Vanessa has made a lot of similar choices for similar reasons, and thought things through carefully, so it would be good to understand some of those choices and compare with my picture. [Here is the next thing I'd read in pursuit of that.](https://www.lesswrong.com/sequences/n7qFxakSnxGuvmYAX)



It could also be interesting to relate this sort of optimality notion to tiling, the CDT vs EDT debate, and other interesting questions. The main reason I include it in this list is because I think the ideas could be related to other projects listed here; I don't have some specific project for the summer in this section, but I wanted to include it nonetheless.

Please develop the formalism from this post: [scrubbed]

There are a few problems with the post I'd like to see addressed.

Ideas I'd most like to make clear, in rough priority order:

* Decision problems can be "inconsistent" (miscalibrated).
* For a decision problem to be well-defined for a proposed decision procedure, we need to be able to consistently place that decision procedure in that decision problem.
* Decision problems shouldn't be thought of as just one decision point (subjective probabilities, subjective utilities, and a set of possible actions). Some decision problems inherently imply the existence of other decision-points. (EG, transparent newcomb should not be thought of as just the empty case in isolation.)
* I want to argue that UDT is not just EDT working at the policy level: to me, UDT and EDT should not be viewed as answering different questions (solving different decision problems). I see UDT as challenging EDT's answer to the same decision problems (transparent newcomb and counterfactual mugging and so on).
* I want to argue that an agent is comprised of many decisions which "cohere" with each other; EG, the heart works in concert with the lungs. Updateful DT's focus on optimality at isolated decision points regardless of the larger decision problem advises an agent against this sort of broader coherence!
* Finally, I would like to argue against the idea that we should accept the impossibility of probability-zero actions. I feel this is deeply related to the issues at hand, though perhaps I haven't made the connection yet. The true argument against this in my mind is spurious proofs; if we accept the principle that we should not take probability zero actions, then there can a proof through Löb's theorem that we don't take any particular action (depending on the order in which we check proofs, and other details of our decision theory). This reasoning might not be the most natural way to frame it within the formalism we're building here, since it invokes logical reasoning, which isn't explicitly represented here. Instead, I think the phenomenon manifests in this kind of framework as "if a decision procedure accepts this principle (to ignore zero-probability actions), then a decision problem can cause you to not take actions you should simply by telling you you won't." A bad example: in transparent newcomb with a perfect predictor and the large box empty, an updateful agent knows that it won't two-box, so it'll one-box. (This is a bad example because both CDT and EDT already one-box for other reasons in this example; a better example would be five-and-ten with a perfect predictor who tells you you'll take five (if and only if you're vulnerable to this).)
A question we should be asking ourselves when writing: what decision problems best illustrate these ideas? In my post, I used commonly-discussed decision problems to make my points, but I doubt these are the best examples to use didactically.

I've attached some notes about ways I would like the formalism to be better. To start, please read the lesswrong post and then make the easy improvements, focusing on improving the mathematical formalism, making sure all the type-signatures make sense, writing out definitions and conjectures and theorems properly.

Now I'm formalizing the decision problem: there's a root node that branches on a fair coin flip, and in each branch the agent faces the same decision point with the same observation label, but the actual consequences differ depending on which branch we're in—this is the counterfactual mugging setup where the agent's action in one world affects outcomes in another world it doesn't observe.

The key issue is how to represent the agent's choice in the unobserved branch: I could record the simulated answer as a counterfactual fact, but that muddies the richness discussion; instead, I'm introducing a null value for the choice coordinate when there's no live decision, so the state space becomes triples of (branch, choice, payoff) where the choice can be pay, refuse, or null—this keeps the H-branch leaves clean with the null marker while preserving the probability structure.

Now I'm setting up two competing decision problems: B₁ where the simulation's choice determines the transfer, and B₂ (the perverse twin) where the transfers are flipped in the H-branch, then computing their expected values under different agent strategies and checking calibration against the masked event generated by the coin flip and transfer coordinates. The parameter q₀ itself isn't constrained by masked calibration since it's just an action probability, but for strict calibration I need P_s(pay) to equal the conditional probability ν(pay | O_T), which means B₁ and B₂ are strictly calibrated only for procedures where q equals q₀. This creates a tension: both procedures are masked-calibrated for any fixed q₀ and any procedure, yet neither can be optimal on both (B₁ needs q = 1, B₂ needs q = 0), and for strict calibration, every procedure has some problem where it fails to be optimal.

The key insight is that C's behavior at d(q₀) depends on q₀ which is baked into the state, so the order matters—I fix q₀ first, then C produces some output probability f(r) = C(d(r))(pay) for each r. This creates a problem: if C has no fixed point in this family, then no problem in the family is strictly calibrated for C, which by the logic would mean C "avoids" criticism—a thumbs-up by default. The key insight is that B₁ and B₂ present the same decision-point, but the optimal action isn't determined by the calibrated-state information available there; the divergence between them lives entirely in the hypothetical branch that O_T-conditioning can't access, so any procedure optimal for both would need information beyond the decision-point—namely, the prior and model. The artifact is above. Structural choices, briefly: procedures become the dependent product $C \in \prod_{d \in \mathcal{D}} \Delta(A_d)$; decision problems become finite query trees, so "oracle access, multiple calls, no source code" are consequences of the type rather than prose stipulations, and the extensional map $\widehat{B}: \mathcal{C} \to \Delta(\mathbb{R} \times \Omega)$ recovers your original signature as a derived object. Calibration comes in four variants (strict, masked, limit, SSC), your "inconsistency" idea gets a schema/instantiation layer (Definitions 14–16, which also turns Soares's slogan into a definition with a probabilistic $\delta$-version), and your five-and-ten-with-a-prophet example is worked out as Proposition 5 — it does exactly the job you wanted, on a single problem calibrated for both the vulnerable and invulnerable procedures, with no Löbian apparatus.

Three things I found while type-checking are corrections or strengthenings rather than restatements, so check them. First, your Transparent Newcomb footnote claims the variants "don't matter much on most theories"; Proposition 6 shows the optimal act at the empty box flips between "predicts the full-case answer" (V1) and "requires one-boxing in both cases" (V2), and your main text states the V1 rule while the UDT argument you give needs V2 — under V1, policy optimality never favors leaving the small box at the empty point. Second, Proposition 8: single-observation Smoking Lesion is inconsistent for *every* procedure, not just CDT; the informative-observation reading is consistent and reproduces the Tickle Defence (Proposition 9). Third, Proposition 4: strict calibration forbids comparing two procedures that act differently at any commonly-reached point, so your option (c)/(b) modifications are preconditions for the comparative methodology itself, not merely EDT accommodations. Separately, a typo: the Geometric-UDT paragraph says "completely updateful" for both endpoints of the spectrum; the second should be "updateless".

Questions before going further: does my V1 reading of your main-text Omega rule match your intent (Corollary 6.1 hinges on it)? Which calibration notion do you want as official, given that masking requires designating a pre-action algebra per problem (Q2)? Why is X restricted to be finite at the beginning? The notation introduced works equally well for arbitrary X... well, so long as X is a measurable space, that is. I suppose finite X have a very obvious representation as a measurable space, while other X must be equipped with a sigma-algebra to resolve ambiguity.

"Standing Assumption (F)" is introduced at a time when it isn't clear -- the objects involved haven't been defined in order to have a restrictive assumption placed on them. Either this belongs as an informal note at the beginning to set expectations about the document (but not dressed up as if it were a formal assumption), or it belongs later on when the terms have been defined in order to be restricted, or both.

I think in order to make a reasonable claim to generality, the definition of subjective state should in fact be jeffrey-bolker with the necessary causal structure, since that is the most widely accepted framework (amongst our target audience, academic decision theorists) in which CDT and EDT can be evaluated apples-to-apples.

Why have the actions and then an injection into events, rather than just treating actions as events in the first place?

Should decision-points be equipped with subjective states (representing the updateful beliefs at that point) or observations (what you'd update on) or both? Kind of a tough question for me. Equipping them with subjective states allows radical-probabilist scenarios. Equipping them with observations seems to suggest a more dogmatic-probabilist picture, where the decision procedure has to construct the updated subjective state from the prior and the observation (if it uses updates). I think I would probably prefer to keep the subjective states in the decision-points and add observations, so that for example a dogmatic-probabilist can have an "epistemology" (a constraint on allowed subjective states, in decision problems) which rejects radical-probabilist scenarios (where the subjective states are not the ones you'd arrive at via bayesian conditioning on the observations). A decision theory can also approve or disapprove of decision procedures. A really radical-probabilist decision problem can keep the observation as top for all decision-points (nothing is learned with certainty).

I remain flexible and uncertain; not trying to impose a decision.

I'll cut the Tarski flourish to avoid decorative confabulation; the labels-are-constants argument in paragraph 3 is the real theorem-shaped thing.

In the tree type, C-dependence has exactly one inlet: branching on sampled answers. States are node labels—constants of the tree—so they cannot depend on C at all. Calibration equates labels with C-dependent statistics, and an equation between a constant and a function of C can only be solved by choosing the constant per-C: that's the external selection, forced by the type, not smuggled in. The principled line is branching-dependence (world on sampled acts, internal, calibration-governed) versus label-dependence (description on the agent, inexpressible internally).

Branching is quantitatively expressive: with k independent probes, reachable dependence on the mixing probability q is exactly the Bernstein polynomials, which are dense in the continuous functions as k→∞—so "fill with probability equal to your mixing probability" is exactly expressible with one probe, while a threshold "fill iff q > 1/2" is only approximate, but no amount of probing lets a label report q. The formalism already knows this: B_P internalizes the finite, pure selection problem via probes whose observation events end up null, and what resists internalization is exactly the mixed/continuum case—label-dependence.

For the relocation alternative: move states out of problems into agents. Query nodes carry (O, A); the agent is (epistemology mapping contexts to states, choice rule mapping decision-points to action distributions). Calibration splits into environment-side honesty—the problem fairness condition that O-conditional statistics about non-agent facts match what's advertised—though I need to be careful here about what the abstract problem stipulates when it doesn't present states directly.

Tim's objection dissolves by relocation: the tree never needed to know q; the agent's self-model does, and producing a self-consistent state is the agent's deliberation. This parallels Skyrms's deliberational dynamics where probability-of-action evolves during deliberation until reaching a fixed point, with ratifiability as the equilibrium concept—explicitly designed for Newcomblike comparisons of CDT/EDT. The iteration happens inside one decision rather than across environmental repetitions, so the normative single-shot frame survives. The epistemology layer does the fixed-point work and pushes toward the "procedures see observations" horn of the fork. The cost is that the problem alone no longer determines behavior, but UDT already conceded that a full agent requires both epistemology and choice rule.

Existence and multiplicity remain open: for arbitrary C, the equation q = C(d(q))(a) may have no fixed point due to discontinuity in argmax procedures—trembles could restore existence, parallel to mixed strategies in Nash equilibrium. When multiple fixed points exist, they can carry different values, so the existential quantifier in Definition 15 versus the universal in Definition 16 is already a substantive choice.

Now I'm weighing which thread to prioritize: stability refinement to prune unstable self-fulfilling announcements, the branch/label principle as the official story, or the relocation refactor. They're compatible but pull the document in different directions. I should also address whether the general question about doing this better is covered by sections 3–5. The length is five moderately long paragraphs, which fits the soft cap. I'll trim ruthlessly while writing and use logical punctuation. No frame break is needed—his self-assessment about introducing another level is accurate. I'm double-checking the factual claims: Kreps & Wilson 1982 on sequential equilibria with assessments and consistency via totally mixed strategies, and Evans & Honkapohja 2001 on E-stability governing least-squares-learning convergence. So the Bernstein characterization holds exactly: reachable probability functions are precisely those expressible in Bernstein form with coefficients in [0,1], which I've now verified constructively by tracking how decision trees with k probes of a given variable d generate convex combinations of conditional probabilities weighted by binomial terms. The claim about single probes realizing exact probabilities checks out, and the definition ensures labels are constants by construction. I should also note his point about predictors being causally downstream—my concession needs to mirror that: the deviation-counterfactual makes prediction covary with action, which classical CDT rejects, so as an objective standard it would render classical CDT miscalibrated by design; I'll keep that as a discussed option. His remark about stochastic functions being more general is already resolved in his own text, so I'll skip elaborating there. Now I'll write this up cleanly in tight paragraphs, aiming for around 700–850 words.

</details>

Housekeeping first. $\Delta_\sigma(X)$ adopted, subscript omitted under convention. On ultrafilters: yes, the identification is exact — for a Boolean algebra $\mathcal{E}$, ultrafilters correspond one-to-one with consistent, complete true/false assignments, equivalently Boolean homomorphisms $\mathcal{E} \to \{\top,\bot\}$; this is the point-set of the Stone space. One caveat for later: "consistent" means consistent under *finite* combinations. If $\mathcal{E}_s$ is a $\sigma$-algebra, ultrafilters need not respect countable joins ($\sigma$-complete ultrafilters are scarce on atomless algebras), so atomless-JB "worlds" decide events only finitely-coherently and $P_s$ transported to the Stone space is finitely additive. Harmless under finite trees; a landmine near Q6. On the causal referent: I agree, and here is the sharp form of the restriction — the tree's only *intrinsic* counterfactual is the deviation $C[d \mapsto a]$, which is policy-intervention; under it, prediction covaries with action by construction, so adopting it as the referent of $\mathrm{sup}_s$ would rule classical CDT miscalibrated in Newcomb *by definition of fairness*, begging the question. Discuss, don't commit, as you say.

On the fixed-point problem. First, the two-level structure you're uneasy about is the same division of labor as equilibrium-versus-dynamics in game theory, and the parallel is close enough to borrow repairs from. A concrete problem is nearly an *assessment* in the Kreps–Wilson sense (strategy profile paired with a belief system), calibration is their *consistency* — which they define via limits of full-support trembles, i.e. Definition 11 independently reinvented — and "who selects the fixed point?" is the standard critique of rational expectations. The static repair in that literature is a *stability refinement* (E-stability, Evans–Honkapohja): privilege fixed points that are locally attracting under the natural adjustment map (perceived law $\mapsto$ actual law), a condition checkable *at* the fixed point. That imports the learning picture's verdicts about which equilibria are reachable without importing its machinery — a candidate answer to "inferior to learning" that concedes the point about dynamics being the real story while keeping the model static.

Second, I think the principled distinction you're asking for can be *derived from the tree type* rather than stipulated. In Definition 4, $C$-dependence has exactly one inlet: branching on sampled answers. States are node *labels* — constants of the tree — so they cannot depend on $C$ at all; yet calibration equates labels with $C$-dependent statistics, and an equation between a constant and a function of $C$ can only be solved by choosing the constant per-$C$. So external selection is forced by the type, not smuggled. The line is then: *branching-dependence* (the world depending on sampled acts) is internal and calibration-governed; *label-dependence* (the description depending on the agent) is inexpressible internally, lives at the abstract level, and its honesty law is the fixed-point condition — ratifiability is to labels what calibration is to branches. Branching is quantifiably expressive: with $k$ independent probes of $d$, the achievable dependence of any event's probability on $q := C(d)(a)$ is exactly the degree-$k$ Bernstein-form polynomials with coefficients in $[0,1]$ (moderate confidence on exactness; I checked it constructively), dense in continuous $[0,1] \to [0,1]$ — so "fill with probability equal to your mixing probability" costs *one* probe, a threshold "fill iff $q > \tfrac12$" is only approximable, and no amount of probing lets a label *report* $q$. Note your formalism already contains the static form of "don't calibrate the selection calls": strict calibration exempts null-observation labels, and the Told-You-So tree internalizes the finite pure-strategy selection precisely through probes whose unchosen candidates end up null. What resists internalization is exactly the mixed/continuum case — corroborating that label-dependence is the residue the abstract level exists to host. This also explains *why* learning collapses the levels: repeated sampling estimates dispositions, so in the iterated limit branching-dependence swallows label-dependence.

Third, an alternative that dissolves Tim's objection by relocation: take the states out of the problem and put them in the agent. Query nodes carry $(O, A)$; an agent is (epistemology: contexts $\to$ states) $\times$ (choice rule: $(d:\mathcal{D}) \to \Delta(A_d)$); calibration splits into environment-side honesty (a fairness condition on problems) and self-knowledge (a rationality condition on agents). Then the tree never needed to know $q$ — the agent's self-model does, and producing a self-consistent state is *deliberation*, for which there's an existing formalism: Skyrms's deliberational dynamics (*The Dynamics of Rational Deliberation*, 1990), where probability-of-own-action evolves within a single decision until a fixed point, ratifiability is the equilibrium concept, and the uncalibrated intermediate states are deliberational stages rather than environmental queries. That's a third road between magic selection and full learning theory, and it slots into the epistemology layer you proposed last message — though it pushes toward the "procedures see observations" horn of the fork I'd leaned against.

Either way, two residual problems deserve to be surfaced rather than papered over: *existence* (for arbitrary $C$, $q = C(d(q))(a)$ has no fixed point — argmax procedures are discontinuous in the state; trembles as an existence-restorer parallels Nash needing mixed strategies) and *multiplicity* (self-fulfilling prophecies give several calibrated instantiations with different values, so the $\exists$ in Definition 15 versus the $\forall$ in Definition 16 is already a substantive selection policy, and $B_P$ is what adversarial selection looks like internalized). Which thread do you want pulled — the stability refinement, the branch/label principle as the official story, or the relocation refactor? They're compatible, but they'd organize the document differently.

## [12] Human — 2026-07-03T07:24:39.328202Z


wrt the question about whether decision procedures are allowed to make use of the observation given in the decision point: UDT needs it. We want to represent UDT, CDT, EDT, and maybe some aspects of FDT, but it's ok if we don't have true counterlogicals for now. 

Another choice we could make would be to include a prior subjective state along with the updated subjective state and the observation, requiring it to be constant across decision problems (so each concrete decision problem comes with a prior). It would obey its own calibration rule (pretty obvious what the rule would be). This would be provided on the general philosophy that we have to provide the data needed by any proposed decision procedure, and UDT requires the prior. It is ugly, but I think it is worth addressing in the text as an alternative. One reason I think it is not necessary is that we can instead have UDT approve of a different decision procedure for each problem (namely the decision procedure that encodes the right prior), and I think this (lack of a universal decision procedure) expresses a real drawback of UDT: it requires data that other decision procedures do not require, although it seems well worth the cost.

In general your discussion here in the chat has raised many interesting points which I'd like to see reflected in the v2 document. I like the style v1 was written in. 

I suppose there need to be some constraints on observations, a little like a form of calibration for them: the observations need to be in the ultrafilter omega that annotates any leaves below them? Like your existing idea about observation-veridical. Well, that's one constraint we might have. That rules out "fooling" an agent via simulation, like subjective state calibration and unlike observation calibration. So it seems like it might be part of the story relating those two. Observation-calibration encourages updatelessness, while subjective-state-calibration instead calls for one to represent those same decision problems through the lens of anthropic uncertainty, eg uncertainty about whether you're the simulation in omega's head. We want to mirror the theorems of jessica taylor's post on memoryless cartesian environments (including the edt version in the comments). This also has implications for the dogmatic-radical axis.

Fork registered — and one consequence worth naming: with $d = (s_d, O_d, A_d)$ and procedures reading $O_d$, your prior-in-the-point alternative and my $\mathrm{UDT}_B$ remark become the two sides of a currying. A theory needing a prior is either a *family* $(P : \text{Priors}) \to ((d:\mathcal{D}) \to \Delta_\sigma(A_d))$ or a single procedure on enriched points $(P, s, O, A)$; same data, moved across the arrow. I'd argue for your preferred option on a more specific ground than ugliness: one job of the formalism is to make theories' *data requirements* comparable, and enriching every decision-point with a prior equalizes those requirements by fiat, hiding exactly the cost you want the framework to exhibit. The "obvious" calibration rule — $P_{\mathrm{pri}}(X) = \nu_{B,C}(X)$, unconditional — is worth stating anyway because of what it implies: it makes the prior carry self-knowledge of the agent's own action statistics (ratifiability recurs at the prior level), and, pleasantly, prior-calibration plus dogmatic epistemology ($P_{s_d} = P_{\mathrm{pri}}(\cdot \mid O_d)$) *derives* strict OC at positively-observed labels rather than assuming it.

On observation constraints: say a query node $q$ is *subtree-veridical* if $\lambda(\ell) \in O_{d_q}$ for every leaf $\ell$ below $q$, and say $B$ *covers* $d$ if a.s. every run whose leaf-world lies in $O_d$ passes a $d$-node. Subtree-veridicality gives $\mathrm{occ}(d) \subseteq \lambda^{-1}(O_d)$; coverage gives the reverse; together $\mathrm{occ}(d) = \lambda^{-1}(O_d)$ up to null sets, and then per-run SSC and strict OC are *the same equation*, since they condition on the same event. So the divergence between your two calibration notions is exactly the measure of that symmetric difference: simulation queries create $\mathrm{occ} \setminus \lambda^{-1}(O)$ ("queried in worlds where the observation is false"), and the Proposition-1 lift to $\Omega' = \mathrm{Leaves}$ is precisely the enrichment that restores veridicality by making "some instance sees $O$" true in the simulating world — which is Taylor's Cartesian move, stated in her own caveat: "we have to isolate Omega's simulation of the agent as a copy of the agent". That's the bridge you conjectured, in one identity.

The theorem transfer then works, and lands on objects already in v1. Her local condition is $\pi(a|o) > 0 \Rightarrow a \in \arg\max_{a'} \sum_s SIA_\pi(s|o)\, Q_\pi(s, a')$ with unnormalized $SIA_\pi(s|o) := [m(s)=o]F_\pi(s)$. Tree version: for $q \in Q_d$ (the $d$-nodes) let $R_q$ = reach probability, $G_q(a) = \mathbb{E}[u \mid \text{reach } q, \text{force } a \text{ at } q \text{ only}]$. Since $V_B$ is multilinear in per-node draws, the chain rule over tied nodes gives $\partial V / \partial C(d)(a) = \sum_{q \in Q_d} R_q G_q(a)$, and first-order necessity at a maximum yields: **$V$-optimal $\Rightarrow$ $\mathrm{supp}\, C(d) \subseteq \arg\max_a \sum_q R_q G_q(a)$** — with $\sum_q R_q = \mathbb{E}_\mu[\#_d]$, the per-occurrence normalizer of Definition 13. So CDT+SIA-optimality is the KKT condition of $V$, per-occurrence SSC is its anthropic bookkeeping, and the unnormalized form handles null observations exactly as she notes. Not sufficient, per her Nash analogy (Conitzer's comment adds Aumann–Hart–Perry 1997 and Korzukhin 2020 for concrete bad fixed points). The EDT+SSA transfer (Oesterheld's comment, minimal-reference-class SSA) is better than I expected: his cancellation — "$P(o \mid \pi_{o\to a})$ is constant in $a$" — holds automatically in trees, because reaching the *first* $d$-node on a path never consults $C(d)$, so $\mu(\mathrm{occ}(d))$ is deviation-invariant even when $\nu(O_d)$ is not. His condition then collapses to $C(d) \in \arg\max_a V_B(C[d \mapsto a])$, which is *literally* the coherence definition from v1 §7. Coherence thereby acquires an external pedigree: it is EDT+SSA ratifiability, the SSA layer cancelling out (Wei Dai and Oesterheld agree in the thread that the anthropic decomposition is "a null operation" mathematically).

This forces an honest refinement of Proposition 7 that I want in v2 rather than papered over. The wedge I proved conditions on the *observation event* $O_E$, whose probability moves under the deviation; the occurrence-conditioned local functional (coherence/EDT+SSA) does not have that pathology and is satisfied by the $V$-optimum. So the anti-compatibilist claim sharpens rather than dies: an updateful evaluator can be locally consistent with UDT, but only by (a) adopting occurrence-conditioning, i.e. buying anthropic uncertainty about being the simulation — SSC's lens, as your post said — and (b) accepting that the local condition is necessary-not-sufficient, so equilibrium selection still lives at the $V$ level. And this pins the dogmatic–radical mapping: the OC side is dogmatic prior-conditioning on world-events; the SSC side is occurrence-conditioning, which survives $O_d = \top$.

Now turning to the ultrafilter question: for a Boolean algebra, the Stone space consists of ultrafilters as Boolean homomorphisms to {0,1}, which are finitely-consistent complete truth assignments. Every Boolean algebra is isomorphic to the clopen algebra of its Stone space, but an ultrafilter need not respect infinite joins that exist in the algebra.

Here's the key tension: an ultrafilter can contain a disjunction ⋃A_n while containing all the negations ¬A_n, because the union of the remaining disjuncts is still nonzero in the algebra, so this is finitely consistent. But σ-complete ultrafilters—those respecting countable joins where ⋁A_n ∈ U implies some A_n ∈ U—are much rarer on atomless σ-algebras. For a σ-algebra of sets, every point x gives a σ-complete ultrafilter (the principal filter of sets containing x), but the question is whether non-point-derived σ-complete ultrafilters exist on something like Borel(ℝ).

The answer turns out to be reassuring for standard Borel spaces: a σ-complete ultrafilter is really just a {0,1}-valued σ-additive measure, and on Polish spaces every such measure is a Dirac measure at a point (this follows from second countability—you can pin down a unique point using a countable base). So for standard Borel spaces, σ-complete ultrafilters correspond exactly to the underlying points. But for abstract σ-algebras like the measure algebra (Borel sets modulo null sets), which is atomless, there are no σ-complete ultrafilters at all. For σ-algebras where we want points that respect countable joins, we need σ-complete ultrafilters, but these may not exist.

The Loomis–Sikorski theorem resolves this: every σ-algebra is a quotient of a σ-field of sets (the Baire sets of the Stone space) by a σ-ideal of meager sets. So worlds are points of the underlying space, but events are identified modulo the ideal—countable operations are computed pointwise up to negligible discrepancy. I'm also noting that the extensional shadow relationship goes both ways: trees embed into extensional functionals (though infinite-family examples require infinitely many queries), and the extensional view forgets the tree structure (many trees map to one functional).

For Definition 8, I'm defining what it means for a realized path through the tree to be observation-veridical (the outcome at the leaf satisfies the observation formula for that query) and action-veridical (the outcome satisfies the action taken), with a subtree-veridical condition that extends this notion. Now I'm defining strict observation-calibration: the agent's beliefs about events must match the conditional probabilities given the observation, and desirability becomes the conditional expected utility rather than a fixed value. This connects to self-knowledge—with deterministic choices and recording at d, the agent assigns probability 1 to its own drawn action. I'm also introducing masked calibration, which restricts these constraints to a designated pre-action subalgebra of events.

Next I'm covering limit-calibration through trembling-hand perturbations: the choice rule gets mixed with uniform randomness at small rates ε, and calibration holds in the limit as ε vanishes. Then prior-equipped decision points, where all queried points share a common prior that must itself be calibrated to the agent's actual action statistics under the choice rule and belief function.

The key tension is whether to equip every point with a prior by default or leave it as an optional parameter. The unequipped design is cleaner for comparing theories' data requirements—adding a prior to every point masks the actual informational cost. I'm noting that with equipped priors, UDT becomes a single procedure satisfying ∃C ∀B optimality, whereas unequipped UDT is a family parameterized by priors with ∀B ∃C quantifier order.

Proposition 1 shows that prior-calibrated, dogmatic Bayesian points are strictly observation-calibrated: the substitution is immediate. Then zero-respecting actions and the start of SSC, which tracks occurrence and frequency of decision points across runs.

Proposition 2 lifts SSC to a cleaner algebra where worlds are principal ultrafilters (leaves of the lifted structure), and crucially, the lifted observation is just the occurrence set itself—this is cleaner than the earlier version because the observation-in-point design eliminates the need for a separate labeling map. The lifted problem preserves the run distribution and value, and lifted states project back to originals. There's a caveat about distinct points with the same occurrence forcing equal lifted states, and the question of whether the lifted problem is "the same" hinges on superconditioning.

Now I'm working through Proposition 3, which bridges OC and SSC: if every d-node is subtree-veridical and the belief system covers d, then the occurrence set matches the set of worlds satisfying the observation, making the per-run SSC equation and strict OC equations identical. The symmetric difference between these sets reveals where simulation queries diverge—they populate worlds where the observation is false but the query occurs, and vice versa. The Proposition 2 lift restores subtree-veridicality by enriching worlds so that "some instance of me observes O" holds wherever the query appears, which connects to Taylor's Cartesian modeling approach. OC handles simulation by conditioning on world-events and leaving hypothetical instances free, while SSC handles it by making the worlds richer.

v2 is up (v1 untouched). On the ultrafilter question, the uniform move is Definition 1: a world is a *2-valued $\kappa$-additive probability* on the event algebra, $\kappa \in \{\mathrm{fin}, \sigma\}$. At $\kappa = \mathrm{fin}$ these are ordinary ultrafilters and Stone guarantees enough of them — with the bonus that any finitely additive state becomes a genuinely $\sigma$-additive Borel measure on the Stone space, since clopens are compact. At $\kappa = \sigma$ they are $\sigma$-complete ultrafilters: for standard Borel algebras these are exactly the underlying points, but abstract $\sigma$-algebras can be *pointless* — the Lebesgue measure algebra has none (split-and-follow argument in Remark 1.2), and the general repair is Loomis–Sikorski: represent as a $\sigma$-field of sets modulo a $\sigma$-ideal, reading satisfaction modulo negligibles. Under finiteness nothing in the document ever needs more than $\kappa = \mathrm{fin}$, so $\kappa$ rides along as a parameter (Q8).

The worry is legitimate, but it should be posed as "which verdicts ought to factor through which quotient", not as a presumption that the representations co-refer.

Here is the justification I can currently give for the asymmetry, in one sentence: branches *generate* statistics, labels *answer to* them — calibration is a truth-condition, and any formalism with a truth-condition treats the described mechanism and the describing constants asymmetrically, the same way model theory treats a structure and a sentence asymmetrically without this being a representational artifact. The type of Definition 6 enforces it: within a concrete problem, the only channel from $C$ to anything is the call, because $C$ is oracle-typed; a label cannot depend on $C$ any more than a formula can depend on its model. So label-dependence is never a second *mechanism* — it is the semantic fact that a description of a world containing the agent must co-vary with the agent to stay true. On this reading your question "why do calls sometimes count and sometimes not" has a sharp answer: calls count when they are in the world being described (tree nodes — occurrences, anthropic weight, calibration constraints), and the "calls" implicit in *selecting* the calibrated instantiation are in the describing, like the cartographer's computations, which are not located in the city even when the map is of the map-maker's own street. And the framework already polices smuggling in the dangerous direction: Proposition 11 is precisely the case of a stipulated dependence (S2) that no branching mechanism can realize given the rest, and the verdict is inconsistency, not accommodation. Labels are not a free slot for dependence; they are checked against what the branches produce.

That said, auditing your mobility worry case by case shows it has real bite in one place. Where a dependence *can* sit on either side — Omega-as-simulator (internal query) versus Omega-as-statistician (label function of $C(d)$) — the two representations agree exactly on $\nu$, $V$, and every observation-calibration verdict for deterministic procedures (one probe of a deterministic $C$ is a plug-in), so the advice-stance layer is invariant under the move; for stochastic procedures they come apart, but the gap is characterized rather than arbitrary (Proposition 4: internalizable dependence is the Bernstein class, uniform closure the continuous functions), and it is not a coincidence that the non-internalizable residue — thresholds, exact-$q$ access — is exactly where Remark 4.2's existence pathologies live: dependences that no interaction can realize are the ones whose stipulation breaks fixed-point existence. The non-invariant layer is $\mathrm{occ}$: simulator and statistician differ on whether an instance of you runs inside the predictor, so SSC, per-occurrence weights, and the whole of §8's anthropic content shift with the representation. My position is that this is registration, not creation, of a difference — the two representations are different claims about the world, and "Transparent Newcomb" as informally told simply *underspecifies* which is meant, which is plausibly why the anthropic literature fights about it. What the framework genuinely lacks is the bridging criterion: a rule saying which physical processes must be modelled as queries. "Anything that instantiates the agent's decision" is the obvious candidate, Oesterheld's [behaviorist bridge](https://casparoesterheld.com/2017/10/22/a-behaviorist-approach-to-building-phenomenological-bridges/) (identify with whatever is perfectly correlated with you) is one articulation, and this is Q3 and Q9 approached from the other end.

There is also a naturalist reading that partially dissolves and partially deepens the asymmetry. Under the advice and design stances the describer stands outside the described, and the mechanism/semantics boundary is clean. Under the naturalist stance the map is in the territory: the agent's self-model is a label that is also physically instantiated, and every honest label-dependence should in principle be cashable into occurrences — self-prediction as literally sampling one's own dispositions, which is Remark 4.4's collapse read as a metaphysical thesis rather than a limit theorem. That suggests a naturalism criterion for the boundary: label-dependence that resists translation into interaction (the Bernstein residue) marks an *idealization* — something no embedded process could do — and a naturalist should treat problems stipulating it the way we treat frictionless planes. The conventional element that genuinely remains is episode individuation: moving dependence from label to branch is internalizing ambient process into the run (the prophet's tree internalizes the equilibrium search), so the boundary sits exactly where we draw "one interaction", no more arbitrarily and no less — which is the same conventionality your moral-algebra spectrum already carries. Does the semantic-asymmetry answer address the version of the worry you had in mind, or were you also pressing on cases where the *agent's own computation* makes calls to itself — deliberation as self-probing — which I've so far kept out of the trees entirely?

No — and the gap is precisely between the algebra and its points. Closure of $\mathcal{E}$ under countable joins does not make its ultrafilters respect those joins: an ultrafilter is only *finitely* consistent, and it can contain $\bigvee_n A_n$ while containing every $\neg A_n$, even when that join exists in $\mathcal{E}$. Concrete counterexample on the most σ-closed algebra there is: $\mathcal{E} = 2^{\mathbb{N}}$. Any free ultrafilter $U$ (extend the cofinite filter; BPI) has $\mathbb{N} = \bigcup_n \{n\} \in U$ but $\{n\} \notin U$ for every $n$ — a decided-everything "world" in which some number is picked yet no number is it. Same phenomenon on $\mathrm{Borel}(\mathbb{R})$: extend the co-countable filter; no such ultrafilter is σ-complete (a σ-complete one would be a $2$-valued σ-additive measure, hence a Dirac $\delta_x$, which fails to contain the co-countable set $\mathbb{R} \setminus \{x\}$) — the dart that lands on no real number. So "full non-finitary consistency" is a property demanded of the *point*, not inherited from the algebra; that demand is what $\kappa$ was carrying.

There is, however, a parameter-free repair that honors your instinct: define a world as a $2$-valued probability respecting *every join that exists in* $\mathcal{E}$ — a completely prime filter, the locale-theoretic notion of point. Then the demand is read off the algebra's own join-structure rather than imposed: plain Boolean algebras with no infinite joins give ordinary ultrafilters; $\mathrm{Borel}(X)$ for standard $X$ gives exactly the Diracs (existing joins in a set-algebra containing singletons are unions — if $B = \bigvee A_i \supsetneq \bigcup A_i$, delete a point of the difference and contradict leastness); the measure algebra stays pointless. $\kappa$ becomes a derived quantity and Definition 1 loses its parameter.

But before adopting it, two costs. First, joins can exist by order-accident without being semantically intended. In the finite–cofinite algebra on $\mathbb{N}$ — finitary in spirit — the join $\bigvee_n \{n\}$ *exists* (the only upper bound is $\mathbb{N}$), so completely prime filters are exactly the principal ones, and the free ultrafilter — the "some unspecified large number" world, which one might positively want for limit or nonstandard modelling — is banned by a supremum nobody meant as a truth-functional disjunction. Which infinite joins are *semantic* (true iff some disjunct is) versus merely order-theoretic is intent, not structure; the general classical device is a pair $(\mathcal{E}, J)$ with a designated family of joins that points and probabilities must preserve (Sikorski treats representations preserving given joins — moderate confidence on the attribution, high on the mathematics), with Stone = designate none, locales = designate all, and $\kappa$ = designate by cardinality. Second, the probability side keeps a stipulation under any policy: demanding $P_s$ respect all existing joins would make it completely additive, and completely additive measures are purely atomic — Lebesgue measure on $[0,1]$ assigns $1$ to the existing join of its measure-zero singletons. So worlds' consistency and probabilities' additivity must be allowed to differ, and the pleasing "worlds are $2$-valued probabilities" uniformity is already quietly using the designation twice.

Now I'm working through Remark 7.1 and Proposition 7, where the proof claims Proposition 3's identity is contained in the recording clauses. Recording requires exactly one d-node per O_d-run with subtree-veridicality of that node. The coverage direction is tricky: every O_d-run passes a d-node, but subtree-veridicality only applies to the node on O_d-runs. A d-node sitting entirely on non-O_d-runs could contribute runs outside O_d. If a node has some leaves satisfying O_d and some not, the O_d-runs through it require full subtree-veridicality, which forces all leaves below it into O_d — contradiction. So every d-node with any O_d-leaf below must be fully O_d, while d-nodes with no O_d-leaves contribute occurrence-runs outside O_d. For Proposition 7, I need ν(a | O_d) = C(d)(a). Conditioning on λ⊨O_d means runs pass exactly one subtree-veridical d-node where the draw is action-veridical and only-via-draw. Within the O_d-event, the recorded action follows C(d) independently. The draw at that node is C(d)-independent of reaching it, and subtree-veridicality ensures once you reach the node you stay in O_d regardless of draw. The conditioning event {λ⊨O_d} equals the union of reach(q) over all-O_d d-nodes, and reach(q) is independent of the draw at q, so the conclusion ν(a | O_d) = C(d)(a) still holds. Recording is actually compatible with additional simulation queries, which is interesting: strict OC rigidity holds even in simulation problems. This is where a decision theory facing a class of problems could be calibrated to a mixture over instantiations, making states wrong in each individual case but right on average, which is exactly Bayesian rationality under model uncertainty. Beyond that, asymptotic or frequency calibration along a sequence of episodes with empirical frequencies is the learning shadow, and approximate δ-calibration could relax the exact equations to allow total variation bounds between the state distribution and the prior. File state verified against your notes (leaf-payoffs are propagated everywhere; the sup material and both forward-reference clusters are as you describe). Taking the σ-algebra question first, since it has a clean answer: yes, with one sharpening. Being a σ-algebra is a *property* of $\mathcal{E}$ (countable meets exist, and in a set algebra they are the intersections); that property makes a canonical designation available, $J_\omega :=$ all countable subfamilies; and "σ-additive probability" is then exactly "probability with respect to $(\mathcal{E}, J_\omega)$". Both directions are short: a $J_\omega$-probability applied to the complemented family of a disjoint union gives $P(\neg B) = \inf_F (1 - \sum_{n \in F} P(B_n)) = 1 - \sum_n P(B_n)$; conversely σ-additivity gives continuity from above along the chain $B_n = \bigcap_{i \leq n} D_i$, which is cofinal among the finite sub-meets. So measure theory bundles a property and a choice: the property enables $J_\omega$, and adopting it is precisely "liking countable additivity" — one can equally take a σ-algebra of sets with $J = \emptyset$ and get the finitely-additive theory (charges). On notation: I'd resist $\Delta_{\mathcal{E},J}(\Omega)$ as *primary*, because probabilities are functions on $\mathcal{E}$ and $\Omega$ can be too small to carry them — on the measure algebra with countable designation, $\Delta(\mathcal{E}, J)$ is nonempty while $\Omega = \emptyset$. What makes $\Delta_\sigma(X)$ honest is that there the events really are subsets of the carrier. Proposal: $\Delta(\mathcal{E}, J)$ primary; $\Delta_\sigma(X)$ retained as the set-algebra instance, with the convention that a σ-algebra carries its canonical designation (so $\Delta_\sigma(X)$ means the countably additive ones, matching standard usage); the world-carried form is available via the Stone transform $X \mapsto \{\omega : X \in \omega\}$ exactly when there are enough worlds.

On the supposition slot: your dissolution is correct and the confusion was of my making — I routed evidential supposition through the operator because Joyce unifies CDT and EDT as two suppositions, but our architecture doesn't want that unification: the state carries *counterfactual structure* as data, and EDT simply never consults it, using $P_s$-conditionals. With that, the total type $(a : \mathcal{E}) \to \cdots$ is unproblematic and my partial-function proposal is withdrawn. On the name, agreed on both counts — "sup" now collides with a section whose official condition is an inf over finite sub-meets. Candidates: $\mathrm{cf}_s$ (plain), $\mathrm{img}_s$ (Lewis's "imaging", but it connotes the specific closest-world construction), $\mathrm{do}_s$ (imports Pearl's graph semantics). I'd take $\mathrm{cf}_s$, read "counterfactual structure".

Coarse counterexample: let the algebra have atoms $\{\text{smoke}, \neg\text{smoke}\}$ only. Then $\mathrm{cf}_s(\text{smoke})$ is forced to $\delta_{\text{smoke}}$ — identical to conditioning — and the whole CDT/EDT disagreement has retreated into $V$: $V_s(\text{smoke})$ is a news value, encoding the lesion correlation through cancer facts the algebra cannot express, and rigidity copies it, hardcoding EDT's verdict. So: probability-only $\mathrm{cf}$ plus rigidity is exactly as expressive as pair-valued $\mathrm{cf}$ when the algebra is rich enough that atoms settle every value-relevant fact (then within-atom revaluation is impossible and the theories can only disagree about where mass goes); below that richness, pair-valued is strictly more expressive, and the shortfall is precisely what CDT-on-a-coarse-algebra needs. Question 1's relativity-to-$\mathcal{E}$ again.

Your rotation argument lands, and can be sharpened into an impossibility: rotations tilt $P$ by an affine function of $V$ (the qualitative shape $P' \propto P \cdot (1 + cV)$; exact transformation family to be pulled from Bolker or Jeffrey before anything is printed — moderate confidence on the formula, high on the mixing), so "the probability component of the supposed state" is not a preference-invariant notion, and probability-only $\mathrm{cf}$ cannot even be *stated* gauge-invariantly — transporting $\mathrm{cf}_s(a)$ to a rotated frame already requires $V^a$. Exercise, if you want it: try to write the transport of a probability-only $\mathrm{cf}$ under rotation and watch where $V^a$ is forced in. The one counterweight: our Definition 2 posits the $(P, V)$ split as primitive rather than deriving it from preferences, and calibration will later pin $P_s$ and $V_s$ to objective statistics *separately* — the objective side is a preferred frame, and it breaks the gauge. So the document could coherently keep probability-only $\mathrm{cf}$ by leaning on that frame; but the pair-valued type is the choice that doesn't depend on frame-breaking, dodges the $\ll$ casuistry, and demotes rigidity from hardwired assumption to named optional axiom.

Proposed ruling, then: $\mathrm{cf}_s : (a : \mathcal{E}) \to$ supposed $(P^a, V^a)$ pairs satisfying the averaging axiom — the first-order truncation of the coinductive type, with iterated counterfactuals deferred by remark — plus two optional named axioms an epistemology may impose: success ($P^a(a) = 1$) and rigidity (where meaningful). If you ratify that, plus the notation proposal from last turn (abstract $\Delta(\mathcal{E}, J)$ only where load-bearing, point-style everywhere spatiality holds), I'll apply the whole §0–§1 queue in one propagation; if the rotation thread deserves more first — e.g. whether *calibration itself* is rotation-covariant, which touches your Geometric-UDT interests — that's worth a turn before anything is frozen.

Remark 4.1 has too much forward-looking stuff that doesn't really help the reader now. After defining decision procedure, you should define our four decision procedures of interest, cdt, edt, udt, and counterfactual udt (the variant of udt which uses counterfactuals). Note that  udt and c-udt have a prior subjective state as a parameter in their definitions, whereas edt and cdt are parameter-free.

It would be good to have a brief remark emphasizing that these are decision procedures recommended by those respective decision theories, though the definition of decision theories will have to wait until after decision problems. Epistemologies will have to wait to be defined later, after decision problems and decision theories. Decision theories take pairs (decision procedure, decision problem) and give thumbs up or thumbs down (is that decision procedure normative in that decision problem). cdt and edt, the decision theories, have single decision procedures which they thumbs-up for all decision problems. An epistemology is a decision theory which only gives thumbs up and thumbs down based on some criteria about the subjective states which appear when a decision procedure combines with a decision problem. A counterfactual theory likewise only checks whether the counterfactuals conform to some criterion. We can give the significant examples of each of these objects.

One substantive deviation from your spec needs your ruling: I could not define UDT and cUDT with *only* a prior state as parameter, and the obstruction is demonstrable rather than aesthetic. With $\rho_d(a) := a$ (act events), Counterfactual Mugging breaks structurally: the act-event "pay" is disjoint from the heads branch, so conditioning on it — and equally any success-obeying supposition of it — gives the heads-side transfer probability zero, and prior evaluation of the act refuses *whatever the prior believes about the predictor*. The material-conditional repair $\neg O_d \vee a$ fails by antecedent reweighting: at self-belief $\tfrac12$ it scores $\tfrac{y-x}{3}$ against $\tfrac{y}{3}$ and refuses, with the verdict drifting as self-belief moves — the antecedent probability decides, not the stakes. What works is an event true exactly where the *disposition* holds ($V_{s^\circ}(\text{would-pay}) = \tfrac12(y{-}x)$ beats $0$, paying iff $y > x$) — and no such event exists in the CM algebra as modelled. So Definition 5 defines $\mathrm{UDT}_{s^\circ, \rho}$ and $\mathrm{cUDT}_{s^\circ, \rho}$ with a *policy interpretation* $\rho : (d : \mathcal{D}) \to A_d \to \mathcal{E} \setminus \{\bot\}$ as second parameter, and Remark 5.2 records the demonstration. The reading I'd offer: $\rho$ is the formal residue of "UDT needs a self-model", sharpening your post's data-cost point — not just a prior, but a prior whose algebra locates the agent's own policy among the events (richness again, Remark 6.1/Q1).

Epistemology: formalized as a decision theory whose verdict is a function of the *realized interaction alone* — queried points, $\mu$, $\nu$, payoffs — never consulting re-runs under deviated procedures; counterfactual theories get the dual gloss (function of the queried $\mathrm{cf}$'s plus deviation statistics). A consequence worth having: under this formalization the §4 calibration senses *are* epistemologies — the section has been defining decision theories all along.

The author's raising a fascinating type-theory question: could the type signature itself encode the structure of which points are consulted and how often, rather than treating the trace as separate output data? He's essentially asking whether we can represent the effectful computation—the oracle calls—as intrinsic to the type system itself, which is a well-explored area in type theory.

The key insight is that the free monad over an effect signature is exactly the right framework here. We could define the problem as a computation in a free monad with operations for querying and chance, which would give us an inductive type capturing the interaction structure directly. But here's the crucial realization: the trees we've been working with *are* already the free monad representation—they're the canonical type-theoretic answer, just presented concretely rather than abstractly. So the trees aren't a compromise away from type theory; they're the proper type-theoretic solution all along.

The relationship between the two types becomes clear through the handler interpretation: there's a monad morphism that takes a tree (a term of the free monad) and an oracle function, then produces the final distribution over outcomes. This structural recursion is exactly what a free-monad fold looks like. The reason a simple function type can't capture the call-tracking is that functions are extensional by default in type theory—two functions with identical input-output behavior are considered equal. But "which queries were made" is intensional information, and the standard way to preserve that while staying extensional at the semantic level is to reify the computation as a data structure whose constructors represent the effects. This is the syntax-versus-semantics distinction: the free monad is the initial algebra, so a tree is "a program that can query," and the extensional functional is one particular interpretation of it.

Now I'm connecting this to classical results in higher-type computability: the dialogue monad and Escardó's work on continuous functionals show that every System-T-definable functional is realized by a well-founded dialogue tree, and the tree witnesses continuity. Our finite trees play the same role—they correspond to functionals that query the oracle finitely many times, which means they depend on only finitely many coordinates of the procedure space. This gives a precise theorem: tree-realizable extensional functionals are exactly the continuous ones with respect to the product topology on procedures, with finiteness enforcing the uniform boundedness needed for continuity.

The functionals arising from trees are even more constrained than just continuous—they're polynomial in the queried coordinates with a specific Bernstein/stochastic-matrix structure, which is exactly what Proposition 4 characterizes for the single-point case. This connects the strictness results in §3 to the probe expressiveness characterization in Prop 4, showing a nice convergence between the dialogue-theoretic and polynomial perspectives.

I should also note the connections to game semantics (Hyland–Ong, Abramsky–Jagadeesan–Malacaria), where types are games and programs are strategies—our problem-versus-procedure setup is literally a two-player game with the tree as the game tree. There's also potential relevance to lenses, optics, and open games (Hedges' compositional game theory), though that might be getting too abstract for this context.

From a coalgebraic angle, trees fit naturally as elements of (co)inductive fixpoints of a functor that combines the observation space, the finitary distribution monad for chance, and dependent pairs for decision points with continuations—the inductive case gives finite trees, the coinductive case handles possibly infinite ones in the Q8 regime.

The answer structure: first, reify the calls as the free monad on the query signature, which is exactly what Definition 6 captures—trees aren't a retreat from type signatures but the canonical type-theoretic representation of tracked computation, and Definition 7's semantics is the unique fold interpreting effects with an oracle. Second, explain why ordinary functions can't capture this: function extensionality means call-patterns are intensional, so the recipe is to make interaction into data via initiality, then recover functions through interpretation—this connects to algebraic effects and handlers, interaction trees in the coinductive setting, and game semantics. Third, there's a deeper mathematical payoff: a theorem relating which extensional functionals admit tree representations, tied to continuity in higher-type computability and the finiteness constraints already present in §3.

On the trace-in-output question he flagged: that's weaker because outputting the trace is just additional data in the codomain while remaining extensional in the input, with nothing guaranteeing the reported trace matches actual calls—the free-monad approach makes calls constitutive by construction instead. This distinction should surface early since he explicitly marked it. There is a standard type-theoretic answer, and it's pleasingly deflationary: the tree *is* the type signature you're asking for. Reify the calls as constructors — take the inductive type
$$\mathrm{Prob} \;:=\; \mu X.\; (\Omega \times \mathbb{R}) \;+\; \Delta(X) \;+\; (d : \mathcal{D},\; A_d \to X),$$
leaf, chance node, and query-with-continuation (a dependent pair: a decision-point together with a map from answers to the rest of the computation). This is the *free monad* on the effect signature $\{\mathrm{query} : (d : \mathcal{D}) \rightsquigarrow A_d\}$ over the probability monad: the type of "programs that may consult an oracle of type $(d : \mathcal{D}) \to \Delta(A_d)$", with consultation trackable *because the only way the type provides to use the oracle is the constructor*. Definition 6 is exactly this $\mu$-type written concretely, and Definition 7's structural recursion is the fold — in effects vocabulary, the *handler* that interprets each $\mathrm{query}$ node by sampling from $C$, which is the canonical map from the syntax ($\mathrm{Prob}$) to the semantics (the extensional functional). So §3's two definitions already stand in the initial-algebra-to-denotation relation; the tree isn't a retreat from type signatures but the standard type-theoretic representation of tracked interaction.

Your parenthetical — "not just outputting the trace" — is the right thing to be suspicious of, and here is the sharp difference. The trace-in-the-codomain type, $\widehat{B}' : ((d:\mathcal{D}) \to \Delta(A_d)) \to \Delta(\mathrm{Trace} \times \Omega \times \mathbb{R})$, is still a bare function of $C$, so the reported trace is *testimony*: nothing in the type ties it to actual consultation, and a constant function reporting a fictitious trace inhabits the type as comfortably as an honest one. The obstruction to doing better with a function type alone is function extensionality — in ordinary type theory a function *is* its graph, and call-patterns are intensional information — and the general recipe when intensional structure is wanted without abandoning types is exactly the reification above: make the interaction data, recover functions by interpretation. The named traditions, with confidence flags: algebraic effects and handlers (Plotkin–Power; Plotkin–Pretnar — high confidence on attribution); *interaction trees*, the coinductive variant used for possibly non-terminating interaction in Coq (Xia, Zdancewic et al., around POPL 2020 — moderately high confidence on the venue), which is precisely the Q8 regime, infinite trees with a.s.-finite runs as a final coalgebra rather than an initial algebra; and game semantics, under which $B$ is an environment strategy, $C$ an agent strategy, and the tree the game.

There's also a theorem-shaped payoff connecting this to what §3 already asserts informally. In higher-type computability, functionals of an oracle are representable by well-founded question–answer trees exactly when they are *continuous* (the dialogue-tree presentation of a Kleene–Kreisel-era fact; Escardó has a crisp modern treatment via "effectful forcing" — moderate confidence on that attribution). Our infinite-family counterexample is literally a discontinuity witness: in the product topology on procedures, every neighbourhood pins down finitely many coordinates, and "payoff $1$ iff $C$ two-boxes everywhere" doesn't factor through any of them. Finiteness of our trees buys more than continuity — dependence on finitely many coordinates, polynomial per coordinate, with the Bernstein-form positivity structure — and Proposition 4 is the exact image-characterization of the fold for one binary point, $k$ probes. So your question has a research-grade sharpening sitting behind it: *characterize the image of the fold*, i.e. which extensional functionals are tree-realizable with how many probes — Proposition 4 generalized from one point to the whole signature.

Since this isn't an edit request: what it would buy the document, if you later want it, is a three-sentence remark in §3 (Definition 6 = free monad on the query signature; Definition 7 = the handler; extensional shadow = image of the fold), the coinductive upgrade named at Q8, and possibly the image-characterization question added to the questions list — each independent of the others. One honest caveat if we ever formalize: $\mathcal{D}$ is a proper class as defined (states contain arbitrary counterfactual structure), so $\mathrm{Prob}$ as written is large, and a set-sized restriction of the point-class would be wanted before the $\mu$ is literal. Worth queueing any of the three, or leave it as conversation?

Now we're moving into Lemma 1, which establishes that the occurrence measure μ_{B,C}(occ(d)) is independent of the choice of C(d), even though the other measure ν_{B,C}(O_d) generally depends on it. The proof starts by expressing occ(d) as a disjoint union. Now I'm verifying the disjointness claim rigorously: two distinct minimal d-nodes can't both appear on the same path since one would be an ancestor of the other, contradicting minimality. And the union works because the first d-node encountered on any path must be minimal—if it had a d-node above it in the tree, that node would appear earlier on the path, contradicting it being first. For the independence part, reaching a minimal d-node q only depends on chance nodes and decision nodes above q that aren't d-nodes themselves, so the probability factors only involve C(d′) for d′ ≠ d, making it independent of C(d).

The key subtlety is that "independent of C(d)" means the probability of reaching d-nodes is the same whether we use policy C or C′ when they differ only at d—this is deviation-invariance at d, which is exactly what we need for the downstream argument. In the V2 example, changing the policy at d_E shifts the probability of ending in an empty-box world, and since d_E is queried on every path, its occurrence probability is constantly 1, illustrating the principle. This lemma is central to Theorem 2 because it ensures the probability of being consulted doesn't change under deviation, so the argmax of the conditional expectation equals the argmax of the value function plus a constant—in tree form, this follows directly from structure rather than requiring assumptions about memoryless environments.

The lemma also clarifies the updateful/updateless divide: conditioning on being consulted is deviation-stable, but conditioning on observing a particular event is not, which is why occurrence-conditioning avoids the myopia that event-conditioning suffers from. The asymmetry explains why "I might be the simulation" behaves updatelessly while "given that I see the empty box" goes wrong.

Now, there's a sharp boundary worth probing: the lemma says the probability of being consulted stays constant, but what about the total number of consultations? In the AMD example, the expected count of d-node visits depends on the policy at d itself—if the first node always branches to a second d-node with probability depending on C(d), then E[#_d] = 1 + C(d)·(reach probability), which clearly varies with the policy. Lemma 1 says two things, and the precision matters: fixing everything about $C$ except its value at $d$, the probability of *being consulted* at $d$ — $\mu_{B,C}(\mathrm{occ}(d))$ — cannot move, while the probability of the *observation-event* — $\nu_{B,C}(O_d)$ — can. So it's deviation-invariance *at $d$*, not invariance in $C$ outright: your answers elsewhere certainly influence whether you're consulted at $d$; your answer *at* $d$ cannot, because by the time it could matter, the consultation has happened. The proof makes that temporal remark static. Call a $d$-node *minimal* if no $d$-node sits strictly above it; then (i) the first $d$-node on any path is minimal (anything above it on the tree is above it on the path, contradicting firstness), so $\mathrm{occ}(d)$ is the union of the reach-events of minimal nodes; (ii) two minimal $d$-nodes are incomparable, and incomparable nodes share no path, so the union is disjoint; (iii) the path to a minimal $d$-node crosses chance nodes and non-$d$ decision nodes only, so each reach-probability is a product with no $C(d)$-factor. Note what the proof does *not* use: calibration, veridicality, payoffs — it's a pure run-semantics fact, which is why it sits before the Calibration block rather than inside it (a placement your in-order reading should find natural). It also survives the shared-seed variant of Remark 3.2, since reaching a minimal $d$-node consumes no $d$-randomness under any semantics where draws happen at arrival; whether it survives Q12's Kakutani-style relaxations is genuinely open, since equilibrium selection has no "arrival time".

Oesterheld's EDT+SSA cancellation needs the probability of the conditioning event to hold still while the action deviates — condition on $\mathrm{occ}(d)$ and the denominator $\mu_{B,C[d\mapsto a]}(\mathrm{occ}(d))$ is constant in $a$ by this lemma, so ranking actions by occurrence-conditioned value is ranking them by $V_B(C[d \mapsto a])$ plus a constant.
